       Residual Ternary Algebra and a modular foundation:
the s-series, the Bridge Series C, and the 35-component law vector
                                             Arvin Hampton
                                                   539 Labs

                                        5 November 2026 (v16)


                                                  Abstract
          Residual Ternary Algebra (RTA, also called Resonant Algebra) is built from three kinds of
   written objects: elementary packaging arithmetic (the ternary map T3 , the residual cardinality
                                                             P        2
   539, the carrier Ores ), the residual series gs (q) = n≥0 q n /(−q s ; q s )n , and the Bridge Series
   C = P7orth − H7 , an orthant-minus-cone sum on the indefinite lattice Z2 with Gram matrix
   ( 27 77 ). This paper collects, with complete proofs, what can be established in tying these objects
   to a modular foundation, and what cannot.
          Part I treats the s-series gs (written gk there): an exact expansion of the coeﬀicients, families
   of zeros, sign laws for even k, and, for every odd k ≤ 33, a parity-dominance theorem with
   complete zero sets (computer-assisted  √      in stated steps). Part II treats the Bridge Series C: a
   ray-class decomposition over Q( 35), radial asymptotics, a Baker-type obstruction to every
   scalar modular or mock residual law, and a vector-valued structure from a Siegel theta lift.
   Part III is new. It proves that the leading radial profile of C lies outside the span V35 of the
   35-component law vector, while the profile of the normalized series q −7/20 C lies in V35 and obeys
   its vector-valued S-law; it gives the complete radial expansion of q −7/20 C and of 105 companion
   series through order t0 at every rational point, with closed formulas, an algebraicity statement
   at order t−1/2 , and an exact decomposition of that coeﬀicient by quadratic class. The final part
   states precisely which links between RTA and this framework are proved, which written RTA
   objects (T3 , Ores , 539, the packaging identities) have no written link to it, and where the shadow
   Ξ would have to enter; the last question is open. Part III also determines the shadow structure   √
   of the 105 companion series: the exact matrix identities M 2 = 35P and (M T )3 = 35 35P ,
   the resulting parity theorem (a weight-one law forces the trivial multiplier and P -odd vectors),
   the ξ-images of the cone completions in terms of three unary theta series, the Laplacian defect
   of the orthant Siegel lift as one product of unary thetas with an exact vanishing criterion, and
   the line terms of the literal series in closed form. It shows that the transformation law of the
   cone completions is exactly the law of the 105 companions, that the completed odd part has
   trivial radial expansion at every rational point, and that the order t−1/2 coeﬀicient equals the
   sum of explicit completion and line terms; at order t0 the coeﬀicient is the sum of line terms
   and an explicit even corner term, and no aﬀine law of weight-one type holds for the latter; its
   Bernoulli-weighted parts obey an exact S-law into Gauss means outside the companion family.
   The corner term is a cone invariant, transported exactly by the automorphs of the lattice; the
   unit acts on the companions as an involution commuting with the S-matrix, and the orbit
   of the corner under the full automorph group generated by the unit and a reflection has two
   elements; the residual of the fixed S-matrix law for real-weight sums, including A2 , satisfies an
   exact x ↔ 1/x symmetry. This version records the October 2026 packaging identities for the
   local map A1 → Ξ → RTA (Section 39.6): outer, path/branch/ψ, and ε → 0+ /W7 identities,
   together with non-local items A1–A12. The Bridge link remains partial: no readiness claim is
   made and the residual question for C remains open. The h-table of item G-A3 is paused with
   an empty schema, item X4 is absent with a stated gap, and no written samples of hhigh      residual exist;


                                                       1
      these are catalogue-only entries, not theorems. This version (v15) promotes to Category A,
      in Section 40, seven local modular theorems with complete proofs: equality of compact and
      ordinary induction; socle reduction to K1 -invariants; the Breuil–Mézard / Colmez dictionary;
      the filtration identity w(θ3 f ) = 10; Breuil’s socle formula for π(r, 0, χ) at every p ≥ 3; the shape
      of irreducible K-representations; and the Brauer-free classification of irreducibles of GL2 (Fp ).
      The p-regular class count is recorded without Category A completeness status. These identities
      do not settle the residual question for C and do not identify the shadow Ξ. This version (v16)
      adds, in Section 41, three Category A Local cite-lifts F1–F3 (weak NP-completeness of the
      residual path problem on an odd-M slice; nonvanishing determinant does not prevent signed
      cancellation; the signed bound by the regular spectral radius), named so as to avoid collision
      with the E8 filter families F1–F3 of Part II. It records that no normalisation enters those cite-
      lifts, with Ress=1 ζK ≈ 0.837679 reserved only as a Hecke-scale
                                                                   √       fallback; defines the full level-1
      parallel-weight [2, 2] cuspidal Hilbert newspace over Q( 35) of dimension 14 = 2 + 2 + 2 + 8
      containing all four narrow class components; records the classical corrections Burgess 3/16,
      conductor 78400 for Y 2 = X 3 − 35X, and Kim–Sarnak 7/64 not for holomorphic weight 2; locks
      the rank-1 classifier D1 on H 2 (F ) ∼ = Q · [sαδ ] with formality F ≃ Q[0] ⊕ Q[−2]; and locks the
      static cell budget Seﬀ with T3 = 539 as cell/hash capacity (Residual 6= Resonant). Claims not
      established—a Petersson formula for Ξ, Kronecker/four-forces maps, and an explicit formula
      for a boundary vector of C—are recorded as such. No claim is made about the twin prime
      conjecture or the Riemann hypothesis.

                                                   9
1     Introduction
1.1   Goal
The aim of this work is to tie Residual Ternary Algebra (RTA) to a foundation: to identify which
RTA objects are governed by modular, mock-modular or vector-valued modular structure, and to
prove each such statement. RTA, as written, has no algebraic axioms. Its objects are (a) elementary
arithmetic of the ternary map T3 and of the residual cardinality 539 (packaging identities, the
equitable partition 539 = 3 · 68 + 5 · 67, the carrier Ores ); (b) the residual series gs ; (c) the Bridge
Series C and its pieces Caxis , Cthird ; and (d) a weight- 32 shadow Ξ and a weight- 12 period-function
candidate, described in words but not defined as explicit series. A “foundation” here means a
theorem placing an object of (a)–(d) inside a structure with known transformation laws under
SL2 (Z).

1.2   What is achieved
(1) For the s-series (Part I): the coeﬀicient expansion of gs , its zero families, sign laws for even s,
    and complete zero sets and residue-class occupancy for odd s ≤ 33. In particular the residue
    law of g7 recorded in RTA follows from the expansion, and its converse holds (Corollary 42.2).

(2) For the Bridge Series (Part II): C and Cthird share the orthant √    remainder P7orth ; up to theta
    and mock pieces this is a partial theta series of one ray class of Q( 35); a Baker-type argument
    excludes every scalar modular or mock residual law, including every scalar W7 slash law; the
    leading radial data carry a vector-valued structure coming from a Siegel theta lift.

(3) For the 35-component law vector (Part III, new): the leading profile of C is I e(7x/20)K20 (x)
    and lies outside V35 ; the normalized series q −7/20 C has profile I K20 ∈ V35 and satisfies the
    vector-valued S-law of V35 ; the radial expansion of q −7/20 C and of 105 companion series is
    computed in closed form through order t0 at every rational point.

(4) For the foundation question (§42): every written link between RTA and the framework above
    is listed with its status.

1.3   What is not achieved
(1) No written object links T3 , Ores , the number 539 or the packaging identities to gs , C or V35 .
    These parts of RTA are not tied to the modular framework by anything proved here.

(2) The shadow Ξ is not identified. The vector-valued law of V35 holds at leading order only; at
    order t−1/2 no law of the same kind is known, and the numerical evidence (Appendix D) is
    against closure inside the companion span.

(3) No link to the gs family is proved beyond the shared definition and the residue law.

(4) Nothing here concerns the twin prime conjecture, the Riemann hypothesis, or any security
    property of a hash construction.

1.4   Status conventions
Every statement in a theorem, proposition, lemma or corollary is Category A: proved here; or
proved by a finite exact computation (integer, rational, modular or cyclotomic arithmetic, including


                                                   10
ranks modulo a prime together with the argument that transfers them to characteristic 0); or proved
by a finite computation in rigorous ball or interval arithmetic (labelled computer-assisted); or proved
conditional on a cited published theorem, named each time. Category B statements (floating-
point evidence, fits, singular-value ranks, patterns, interpretations) appear only in remarks labelled
“Remark (Category B)”, in status boxes marked Category B, or in the appendices. Part II uses
status boxes after its results; their wording is part of the record.

1.5   Organisation
Section 2 fixes common notation and resolves notational conflicts between the parts. Part I (Sec-
tions 3–8) is the s-series. Part II (Sections 9–32) is the Bridge Series. Part III (Sections 33–36) is
the 35-component law vector and the normalized Bridge Series. Section 42 treats the foundation
question and Section 43 lists open problems. Appendices A–C contain the computational records
of Parts I–II, and Appendix D the Category B record of Part III.

   The series for gk converges in Z[[q]] because the n-th summand is q n Pn (q k ) and has order n2 .
   Proposition 3.2 (expansion in d). For all k ≥ 1 and d ≥ 0,
                                             X                  
                                                          d − n2

                                                2        P               2
    Proof. The n-th summand of gk (q) is q n Pn (q k ) = m≥0 ϕn (m)q n +km . The coeﬀicient of q d
collects the pairs (n, m) with n2 + km = d. ■
    The summand of index n in (3.1) is called the term n of gk (d); it is defined when n2 ≤ d and
k | d − n2 , and mn = (d − n2 )/k is its index.
    Definition 3.3. Let Sk = { n2 mod k : n ∈ Z } ⊆ Z/kZ. An integer d ≥ 0 is allowed for k if
d mod k ∈ Sk .
                              Zk = { d ≥ 0 allowed for k : gk (d) = 0 }.
OccFiltk (∞) is the set of residue classes r ∈ Z/kZ such that gk (d) 6= 0 for all suﬀiciently large
d ≡ r (mod k).
     Proposition 3.4. (i) If d is not allowed for k then gk (d) = 0. (ii) OccFiltk (∞) ⊆ Sk , with
equality if and only if Zk is finite.
     Proof. (i) If d mod k ∈
                           / Sk , no n satisfies n2 ≡ d (mod k), so the sum (3.1) is empty. (ii) A class
r∈ / Sk has gk (d) = 0 for all d ≡ r, hence r ∈/ OccFiltk (∞). For r ∈ Sk , r ∈ OccFiltk (∞) if and only
if Zk contains only finitely many d ≡ r. Since there are finitely many classes, equality holds if and
only if Zk is finite. ■
     By Proposition 3.4, the statement OccFiltk (∞) = Sk (“every square class occurs for all large
d”) is the statement that Zk is finite. Section 8 proves it for every odd k ≤ 33, with Zk computed
exactly.

4.2   The case of odd k
Proposition 4.7 (E–O identity). Let k be odd and d ≥ 0. For each term n of gk (d) with index
mn , (−1)d ϕn (mn ) = (−1)n an (mn ). Hence
                                                 X                X
               (−1)d gk (d) = Ed − Od ,     Ed =   an (mn ), Od =    an (mn ),          (4.4)
                                                    n even                    n odd

the sums running over the terms n of gk (d); all summands are ≥ 0.
    Proof. d = n2 + kmn with n2 ≡ n and kmn ≡ mn (mod 2) (k odd), so (−1)d = (−1)n+mn . By
(4.2), (−1)mn ϕn (mn ) = an (mn ). Multiply: (−1)d ϕn (mn ) = (−1)n an (mn ). Summing over n and
using (3.1) gives (4.4). ■
    In particular, for odd k and allowed d: gk (d) 6= 0 if and only if Ed 6= Od , and the term of index
n has sign (−1)d+n .


5     Zero families
5.1   Squarefree k
                                                            
Theorem 5.1. If k ≥ 2 is squarefree, then gk k(k + 2) = 0.
    Proof. Let d = k 2 + 2k. A term n of gk (d) has n2 ≤ d < (k + 1)2 , so n ≤ k, and k | n2 . Since
k is squarefree, k | n2 implies k | n, so n ∈ {0, k}. For n = 0 the index is m = k + 2 ≥ 1 and
ϕ0 (k + 2) = 0 (Lemma 4.6). For n = k the index is m = 2 ≤ k, so ϕk (2) = c2 = 0 (Lemma 4.1,
Lemma 4.3). Hence gk (d) = 0. ■
    Remark 5.2. The hypothesis k ≥ 2 is needed: g1 (3) = ϕ1 (2) = 1. Exact evaluation of gk (k(k+2))
for every k = 2, . . . , 1001 gives 0 for all 608 squarefree k and a nonzero value for each of the 392
non-squarefree k (for k ≤ 30 non-squarefree: g4 = −1, g8 = 1, g9 = 2, g12 = −2, g16 = −2, g18 = 6,
g20 = −5, g24 = 6, g25 = 6, g27 = 8, g28 = −8 at d = k(k + 2)). This is a computation (Appendix
A.1).

5.2   Values at d = k 2 + jk + b2
Theorem 5.3. Let b ∈ {1, 2}, j ∈ Z, and let k = pa be an odd prime power with

                                 k ≥ K := max(j + 3b, 2b + 1, −j).

Then for d = k 2 + jk + b2 ,

                        gk (d) = ϕb (k + j) + [ j ≥ −2b ] cj+2b + [ j ≥ 2b ] cj−2b ,              (5.1)

where [·] is the Iverson bracket and cm is as in Lemma 4.3 (with cm for m ≥ 0 only occurring when
its bracket is 1).
    Proof. Let n be a term of gk (d): n2 ≡ d ≡ b2 (mod pa ), so pa | (n − b)(n + b). A common prime
factor of n − b and n + b divides 2b ∈ {2, 4}, hence is not p. Thus pa divides one of the two factors:

                                                    16
n ≡ ±b (mod k). The candidates are n ∈ {b, k − b, k + b, 2k − b, 2k + b, . . . } (as k > 2b, these are
distinct and increasing in this order, and b ≥ 1).
    Large n do not occur. For n ≥ 2k − b we would need (2k − b)2 ≤ d, i.e. 3k ≤ j + 4b. But
k ≥ j + 3b gives j + 4b ≤ k + b, and 3k ≤ k + b contradicts k ≥ 2b + 1.
    The three remaining candidates.

   • n = b: b2 ≤ d iff k + j ≥ 0, true since k ≥ −j. Index m = (d − b2 )/k = k + j; the term is
     ϕb (k + j).
   • n = k − b (≥ 1): (k − b)2 ≤ d iff j ≥ −2b. Index m = (d − (k − b)2 )/k = j + 2b. Since
     k ≥ j + 3b, m = j + 2b ≤ k − b = n, so the term is cj+2b (Lemma 4.1).
   • n = k + b: (k + b)2 ≤ d iff j ≥ 2b. Index m = j − 2b, and m ≤ n since k ≥ j + 3b; the term is
     cj−2b .

   Summing gives (5.1). ■
   Corollary 5.4 (three families). Let k be an odd prime power.
                     
   1. gk (k + 2)2 − 3 = 0 for every k ≥ 5. For k = 3: g3 (22) = −1.
   2. If k ≡ 3 (mod 4): gk (k 2 + 2k + 4) = 0 for every k 6= 3, 7. Values: g3 (19) = −1, g7 (67) = +1.
   3. If k ≡ 1 (mod 4): gk (k 2 + 3k + 4) = 0 for every k 6= 5. Value: g5 (44) = −1.

     Proof. (1) (k+2)2 −3 = k 2 +4k+1: b = 1, j = 4, K = 7. By (5.1), for odd k ≥ 7: g = ϕ1 (k+4)+
c6 + c2 = (−1)k+4 + 1 + 0 = 0. For k = 5 the terms are (n, m,  P ϕn (m)) = (1, 9, −1), (4, 6, +1), (6, 2, 0)
(the term n = 4 has m = 6 > 4; by (4.3), ϕ4 (6) = c6 + 1x=0 (−1)x Q(x) = 1 + (1 − 1) = 1), so
g5 (46) = 0. For k = 3: d = 22, terms (1, 7, −1), (2, 6, 0), (4, 2, 0), so g3 (22) = −1.

 (2) k 2 +2k+4: b = 2, j = 2, K = 8. For k ≥ 9, (5.1) gives g = ϕ2 (k+2)+c6 = ϕ2 (k+2)+1, which
     is 0 iff ϕ2 (k + 2) = −1 iff k + 2 ≡ 1 (mod 4) (Lemma 4.6) iff k ≡ 3 (mod 4). The odd prime
     powers k ≡ 3 (mod 4) below 9 are 3, 7. For k = 7: d = 67, terms (2, 9, −1), (5, 6, +2) [ϕ5 (6) =
     c6 + Q(0) = 2], so g7 (67) = +1. For k = 3: d = 19, terms (1, 6, +1), (2, 5, −1), (4, 1, −1), so
     g3 (19) = −1.

 (3) k 2 + 3k + 4: b = 2, j = 3, K = 9. For k ≥ 9, (5.1) gives g = ϕ2 (k + 3) + c7 = ϕ2 (k + 3) − 1,
     which is 0 iff ϕ2 (k + 3) = 1 iff k + 3 ≡ 0 (mod 4) iff k ≡ 1 (mod 4). The only odd prime
     power k ≡ 1 (mod 4) below 9 is 5: d = 44, terms (2, 8, +1), (3, 7, −2), so g5 (44) = −1. ■

    (The term lists were computed by exact integer arithmetic. For all odd prime powers k ≤ 1001
the exact values of gk at these d were also computed directly: the only nonzero values are those
listed. Appendix A.1.)
    Theorem 5.5 (zero classes for b ∈ {1, 2}). Let b ∈ {1, 2}, j ∈ Z, and k ≥ K an odd prime
power as in Theorem 5.3. Then gk (k 2 + jk + b2 ) = 0 in exactly the following cases.

   • b = 1: j ∈ {−2, −1, 1, 4} (all k).
   • b = 2, −4 ≤ j ≤ 3: k ≡ 1, 3, 1, 1, 1, 3, 3, 1 (mod 4) for j = −4, −3, −2, −1, 0, 1, 2, 3 respec-
     tively.
   • b = 2, j ≤ −5: k ≡ 1 (mod 4) if j ≡ 1, 2 (mod 4) and k ≡ 3 (mod 4) if j ≡ 0, 3 (mod 4).
   • b = 2, j ≥ 4: never.

   Proof. Let k be odd. We use (5.1) and the values c0 , . . . , c7 = 1, −1, 0, −1, 1, −1, 1, −1 and
cm = (−1)m Q(m).


                                                    17
     b = 1. ϕ1 (k + j) = (−1)k+j = −(−1)j . For j ≤ −3 only this term is present, so g = ±1 6= 0. For
−2 ≤ j ≤ 1: j = −2: −1 + c0 = 0; j = −1: +1 + c1 =        0; j = 0: −1 + c2 = −1; j = 1: +1
                                                                                            + c3 = 0.
For j ≥ 2: g = −(−1)j + (−1)j Q(j + 2) + Q(j − 2) = (−1)j Q(j + 2) + Q(j − 2) − 1 (here cj+2
and cj−2 have the sign (−1)j ). This vanishes iff Q(j + 2) + Q(j − 2) = 1. Since Q(j + 2) ≥ 1 and
Q(j − 2) ≥ 1 unless j − 2 = 2 (Lemma 4.4), this holds iff j = 4 (Q(6) + Q(2) = 1 + 0). For j = 2, 3:
Q(4) + Q(0) = 2, Q(5) + Q(1) = 2, nonzero.                                                     
     b = 2, j ≥ 4. ϕ2 (k + j) ∈ {1, −1, 0, 0} and cj+4 + cj−4 = (−1)j Q(j + 4) + Q(j − 4) with
Q(j + 4) ≥ 2 (j + 4 ≥ 8) and Q(j − 4) ≥ 0, so |cj+4 + cj−4 | ≥ 2 > |ϕ2 (k + j)|: g 6= 0.
     b = 2, −4 ≤ j ≤ 3. Here g = ϕ2 (k + j) + cj+4 , which vanishes iff ϕ2 (k + j) = −cj+4 . For
j + 4 = 0, . . . , 7 we have −cj+4 = −1, 1, 0, 1, −1, 1, −1, 1. By Lemma 4.6, ϕ2 (m) = −1 iff m ≡ 1,
= 1 iff m ≡ 0, = 0 iff m ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4). So: j = −4: k − 4 ≡ 1, k ≡ 1. j = −3: k − 3 ≡ 0, k ≡ 3.

k + 1 ≡ 0, k ≡ 3. j = 2: k + 2 ≡ 1, k ≡ 3. j = 3: k + 3 ≡ 0, k ≡ 1.
     b = 2, j ≤ −5. Then j < −2b and j < 2b, so g = ϕ2 (k + j), zero iff k + j ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4). With
k odd: j even requires k + j ≡ 3, i.e. k ≡ 3 − j; j odd requires k + j ≡ 2, i.e. k ≡ 2 − j. These give
k ≡ 1 for j ≡ 2, 1 and k ≡ 3 for j ≡ 0, 3 (mod 4). ■
     (For b = 2 and j ≤ −5, the condition k ≥ K reads k ≥ −j.) The cases (b, j) = (1, 4), (2, 2),
(2, 3) are the three families of Corollary 5.4. Appendix A.1 lists the exact values of (5.1) for all
(b, j) with −8 ≤ j ≤ 16 and all odd prime powers k ≤ 500 with k ≥ K against the exact coeﬀicients
of gk : 5121 cases, no discrepancy.


6     The value of gk at (k + 2)2 − 3 for odd k with two prime factors
Throughout this section dk = (k + 2)2 − 3 = k 2 + 4k + 1.

6.1    Reduction to one pair of terms
For a positive integer s and an integer k > 2s with k | s2 − 1 put

                s2 − 1
           t=          ,   m1 = k + 4 − t,   m2 = 4 + 2s − t,       n2 = k − s,   u = k − 2s,

(so m1 − m2 = u).
    Lemma 6.1 (pair reduction). Let k ≥ 7 and Ξk = {s : 1 < s < k/2, s2 ≡ 1 (mod k)}.
Then                                        X                      
                     gk (dk ) = (−1)k + 1 +    ϕs (m1 ) + ϕk−s (m2 ) ,          (6.1)
                                                   s∈Ξk

and ϕs (m1 ) = (−1)m1 A1 , ϕk−s (m2 ) = (−1)m2 A2 . For odd k the bracket equals (−1)t+1 (A1 − A2 ).
   Proof. A term n of gk (dk ) has n2 ≤ k 2 + 4k + 1 < (k + 2)2 , so n ≤ k + 1, and n2 ≡ dk ≡ 1
(mod k). The residues r ∈ {0, . . . , k − 1} with r2 ≡ 1 include 1 and k − 1 (r = 0 is excluded since
k ≥ 2), and the others 1 < r < k − 1 come in pairs {s, k − s} with s ∈ Ξk (s = k/2 is not a root for
k ≥ 4: if k = 2h with h odd then h2 ≡ 1 mod 2h forces h = 1, and if 4 | k then (k/2)2 ≡ 0 mod k).
For n ≤ k + 1 the possible n are therefore n = 1, n = k − 1, n = k + 1, and n ∈ {s, k − s}, s ∈ Ξk
(note r + k > k + 1 for r ≥ 2).

    • n = 1: m = (dk − 1)/k = k + 4; term ϕ1 (k + 4) = (−1)k .


                                                    18
                                             
  • n = k − 1: m = k 2 + 4k + 1 − (k − 1)2 /k = 6 ≤ k − 1, so the term is c6 = 1 (Lemma 4.1,
    4.3).                                   
  • n = k + 1: m = k 2 + 4k + 1 − (k + 1)2 /k = 2; the term is c2 = 0.
  • n = s: m = (k 2 + 4k + 1 − s2 )/k = k + 4 − t = m1 ; term ϕs (m1 ).
  • n = k − s: m = k 2 + 4k + 1 − (k − s)2 /k = 4 + 2s − t = m2 ; term ϕk−s (m2 ).

    This is (6.1). By (4.2), ϕn (m) = (−1)m An (m). For odd k: m1 ≡ 1 + t and m2 ≡ t (mod 2), so
the bracket is (−1)t+1 A1 + (−1)t A2 . ■
    Lemma 6.2 (root geometry). Let k be odd, s ≥ 3, k > 2s, k | s2 − 1. Then: (i) √ 1 ≤ t < s/2;
(ii) u = k − 2s is odd and u ≥ 3, u2 ≥ k + 4, m1 ≤ u2 − 1 and hence u ≥ m1 + 1; (iii)
2 s + 4 < m2 ≤ 2s + 3 and m2 < m1 ; (iv) n2 ≥ s + 3, and with y2 = m2 − n2 − 1: y2 ≤ s − 1
3

whenever m2 > n2 .
    Proof. (i) t ≥ 1 since s2 − 1 ≥ k > 0 is a positive multiple of k; and k > 2s gives t <
(s2 − 1)/(2s) < s/2. (ii) u is odd since k is odd. u ≡ −2s (mod k) gives u2 ≡ 4s2 ≡ 4 (mod k).
If u = 1 then k | −3, i.e. k = 3, contradicting k > 2s ≥ 6. So u ≥ 3, u2 − 4 > 0 is a positive
multiple of k, whence u2 ≥ k + 4. Then m1 = k + 4 − t ≤ k + 3 ≤ u2 − 1. (iii) m2 = 2s + 4 − t ≤
2s + 3 and m2 > 2s + 4 − s/2. m1 − m2 = u > 0. (iv) k = 2s + u ≥ 2s + 3. If m2 > n2 :
y2 = (2s + 4 − t) − (k − s) − 1 = s + 3 − t − u ≤ s + 3 − 1 − 3. ■

6.2   Estimates for A1 and A2
Lemma 6.3. Let s ≥ 3, k odd, k > 2s, k | s2 − 1, and assume that Q is nondecreasing on [2, ∞)
(Lemma 6.5 below).
                                               m−s+1
  1. For every m ≥ s + 4: As (m) ≥ Q(m) −                  Q(m − s − 1).
                                                    2
  2. Let e0 = s + 1 if s is odd and e0 = s + 2 if s is even. If D ≥ 0 is a sum of integers from the
     set {e even : s < e ≤ 2s}, then As (m) ≥ As (m − D). A number D is such a sum if D = 0 or
     if D is even, D ≥ e0 and: (s odd) always; (s even, s ≥ 4) D 6= 2s + 2.
  3. A2 ≤ Q(m2 ) + Q(s), and A2 = Q(m2 ) if m2 ≤ n2 .
  4. If m1 ≥ 5s + 8, then n2 > m2 and A2 = Q(m2 ) ≤ Q(2s + 3).

   Proof. (1) By (4.2), As (m) is at least the number of partitions of m into distinct odd parts all
≤ s (take µ = ∅). P
                  The number of partitions of m into distinct odd parts having at least one part
j > s is at most j odd, s<j≤m Q(m − j) (remove the part j). The arguments x = m − j have
the parity of m − 1 and satisfy 0 ≤ x ≤ y := m − s − 1. For y ≥ 3, Q(x) ≤ Q(y) for 2 ≤ x ≤ y
(monotonicity) and for x ∈ {0, 1} (as Q(x) = 1 ≤ Q(y), Lemma 4.4). The number of such x is at
most by/2c + 1 ≤ (m − s + 1)/2. Hence As (m) ≥ Q(m) − m−s+1   2   Q(m − s − 1).

 (2) By (4.2), adjoining a fixed multiset of even parts in (s, 2s] to µ is an injective map from the
     pairs counted by As (m − D) to those counted by As (m). For j parts, the attainable sums
     are exactly all even numbers in [je0 , 2js] (each part ranges over all even integers in [e0 , 2s]).
     For s odd, e0 = s + 1, and consecutive intervals overlap or touch: (j + 1)(s + 1) ≤ 2js + 2
     iff s − 1 ≤ j(s − 1). So every even D ≥ s + 1 is attainable. For s ≥ 4 even, e0 = s + 2: for
     j = 1, 2 the intervals are [s + 2, 2s] and [2s + 4, 4s], missing only 2s + 2 between them; for
     j ≥ 2, (j + 1)(s + 2) ≤ 2js + 2 iff s(j − 1) ≥ 2j, true for s ≥ 4.

 (3) By Lemma
            P 2 6.2 (iv), n2 ≥ s + 3 so m2 ≤ 2s + 3 ≤ 2n2 + 1 and (4.3) applies: A2 = Q(m2 ) +
     (−1)m2 yx=0  (−1)

                                   2     2   2                         2    2

     by monotonicity. ■

6.4   The inequality A1 > A2
Theorem 6.6. Let k be odd, s ≥ 3, k > 2s, k | s2 − 1. Then A1 > A2 , except for (s, k) = (4, 15)
and (8, 21), where A1 = A2 = 2 and A1 = A2 = 6 respectively.
    The proof has two parts: a finite exact computation for 3 ≤ s ≤ 2500 (Proposition 6.7) and an
analytic argument for s ≥ 2501 (Proposition 6.8).
    Proposition 6.7 (finite range; computer-assisted). For every pair (s, k) with 3 ≤ s ≤
2500, k odd, k > 2s, k | s2 − 1 (there are 15 616 pairs), A1 > A2 except for (4, 15) and (8, 21).
    Proof. All arithmetic is exact integer arithmetic. The table of Q(0), . . . , Q(35200) is computed
exactly. For each pair, A2 = An2 (m2 ) is computed by (4.3) (valid since m2 ≤ 2n2 + 1, Lemma 6.2).
A lower bound LB ≤ A1 is computed as follows: choose m′′ ≤ m1 with m′′ ≡ m1 (mod 2) such
that D = m1 − m′′ is a sum of even integers in (s, 2s] (this is decided exactly by the criterion of
Lemma 6.3(2): D = 0, or D even and dD/2seeP                                       ′′
                                                 0 ≤ D), and set LB = As (m ) computed by (4.3)
     ′′                                     ′′                        ′′
if m ≤ 2s + 1, and otherwise LB = Q(m ) − j odd, s<j≤m′′ Q(m − j). By Lemma 6.3(2) and
the proof of Lemma 6.3(1), LB ≤ As (m′′ ) ≤ A1 (the inequality Q(x) ≤ Q(y) is not used here: the
exact sum is subtracted). The candidates are the integers m′′ with m2 < m′′ ≤ min(m1 , 35200)
satisfying the two conditions above (they are tried in the order m1 , 4s + 3, 4s + 2, 4s + 1, 4s, then
downward from min(m1 , 8s, 35200); the order only affects speed), and the first with LB > A2 is
accepted. The result: 15 587 pairs are certified (LB > A2 , so A1 > A2 ). The remaining 29 pairs
all have s ≤ Q 32; they are listed
                              Q in the output file and are settled by the exact computation of
A1 = [xm1 ] j odd≤s (1 + xj ) s/2<i≤s (1 − x2i )−1 (Lemma 4.2), which was carried out for all pairs
with s ≤ 300 (995 pairs): A1 > A2 for every pair except (4, 15) and (8, 21); the smallest ratio
A1 /A2 among the others is 1.7811 at (s, k) = (34, 77). For the two exceptions the terms of gk (dk )
are listed in Section 6.6; they give A1 = A2 = 2 for (4, 15) and A1 = A2 = 6 for (8, 21). ■

6.5   The theorem
Theorem  6.9. Let k be an odd integer with exactly two distinct          prime
                                                                               factors. Then gk (k +
2)2 − 3 6= 0, except for k = 15 and k = 21, for which
                                                        g k (k + 2) 2 − 3 = 0.

    For odd prime powers k ≥ 5, gk (k + 2)2 − 3 = 0 (Corollary 5.4).
    Proof. Let k = pa q b with p < q odd primes; k ≥ 15. The solutions of x2 ≡ 1 (mod k) are, by
the Chinese remainder theorem, exactly four residues ±1, ±s0 with s0 6≡ ±1. Hence Ξk consists of
exactly one element s (one of s0 , k − s0 lies in (1, k/2)), and s ≥ 3 since s2 ≡ 1, s = 2 would give
k | 3. By Lemma 6.1 and (−1)k + 1 = 0,
                                                
                               gk (k + 2)2 − 3 = (−1)t+1 (A1 − A2 ).

By Theorem 6.6, A1 6= A2 unless (s, k) ∈ {(4, 15), (8, 21)}, and for these two pairs A1 = A2 , so
gk (dk ) = 0. ■

6.6   The cases k = 15 and k = 21
For k = 15, d = 286: the terms (n, m, ϕn (m)) are (1, 19, −1), (4, 18, +2), (11, 11, −2), (14, 6, +1),
(16, 2, 0) (s = 4, t = 1, A1 = A4 (18) = 2, A2 = A11 (11) = 2). For k = 21, d = 526: (1, 25, −1),
(8, 22, +6), (13, 17, −6), (20, 6, +1), (22, 2, 0) (s = 8, t = 3, A1 = A8 (22) = 6, A2 = A13 (17) = 6).
In both cases the sum is 0.
    Computer-assisted items in this section: the table of Q up to 35200 and its monotonicity; the
interval evaluation of CE , K, β̄ and of the margins at s = 2501; the finite certificate of Proposition
6.7 (Appendix A.2).

7      Even k
7.1     Signs of the terms and finiteness of the zero set in “one-sign” classes
Lemma 7.1. Let k ≥ 1 and d ≥ 0. The term n of gk (d) equals (−1)mn An (mn ). Two terms n, n′
of gk (d) have the same sign factor (−1)mn = (−1)mn′ if and only if n2 ≡ n′2 (mod 2k).
    Proof. The first statement is (4.2). Also mn − mn′ = (n′2 − n2 )/k, which is even if and only if
2k | n′2 − n2 . ■
    Definition 7.2. A residue class c ∈ Sk is a one-sign class if n2 ≡ n′2 (mod 2k) whenever
n2 ≡ n′2 ≡ c (mod k). For such a class and every d ≡ c (mod k), all terms of gk (d) have the same
sign factor εd = (−1)mn and
                        X
            gk (d) = εd   An (mn ),     so gk (d) 6= 0 ⇐⇒ An (mn ) ≥ 1 for some term n.       (7.1)
                            n

    Lemma 7.3. If k ≡ 2 (mod 4), every class c ∈ Sk is a one-sign class.
    Proof. Let k = 2h, h odd, n2 ≡ n′2 (mod k). Then n ≡ n′ (mod 2), so n2 −n′2 = (n−n′ )(n+n′ )
is a product of two even numbers and 4 | n2 − n′2 ; also h | n2 − n′2 . As gcd(4, h) = 1, 4h = 2k
divides n2 − n′2 . ■
    Theorem 7.4. Let k ≥ 2 and let c be a one-sign class. Let n0 be the least integer n ≥ 3 with
n ≡ c (mod k), L = bn0 /2c + 1, and
  2

                                                             
                                   Bc = n20 + k 2L(L − 1) + 1 .

Then gk (d) 6= 0 for every d ≡ c (mod k) with d ≥ Bc , and gk (d) has the sign εd .
    Proof. n0 exists since if n2 ≡ c then also (n + k)2 ≡ c and one of n, n + k, n + 2k is ≥ 3. Let
d ≥ Bc . Then n0 is a term of gk (d), with index m = (d − n20 )/k ≥ 2L(L − 1) + 1. By (7.1) it suﬀices
to show An0 (m) ≥ 1.
    The even integers 2L and 2L + 2 lie in (n0 , 2n0 ]: 2L = 2bn0 /2c + 2 > n0 and 2L + 2 ≤ 2n0 iff
bn0 /2c + 2 ≤ n0 , true for n0 ≥ 3. Every integer N ≥ L(L − 1) is of the form aL + b(L + 1) with
a, b ≥ 0: write N = qL + r, 0 ≤ r < L; then q ≥ L − 1 ≥ r and N = (q − r)L + r(L + 1). Hence
every even integer ≥ 2L(L − 1) is a sum of parts 2L and 2L + 2. If m is even, take λ = ∅ and µ
such a multiset with sum m (m ≥ 2L(L − 1) + 2). If m is odd, take λ = {1} (1 ≤ n0 ) and µ with
sum m − 1 ≥ 2L(L − 1). By (4.2), An0 (m) ≥ 1. ■
    Corollary 7.5. If k ≡ 2 (mod 4) then Zk is finite, Zk ⊂ [0, maxc Bc ), and OccFiltk (∞) = Sk .
    The sets Zk for k ≡ 2 (mod 4), k ≤ 34, are computed exactly in Appendix B.2 (d < maxc Bc is
a finite range; the program evaluates gk (d) exactly for all d ≤ maxc Bc + 3000). For example:

                       Z2 = {2, 8, 10, 26},       Z6 = {3, 6, 12, 18, 21, 24, 30, 46, 48, 78},

                      Z10 = {5, 6, 10, 15, 20, 24, 29, 30, 34, 40, 45, 50, 60, 70, 80, 90, 120},


    Z14 = {2, 7, 8, 11, 14, 21, 22, 28, 32, 35, 37, 42, 44, 46, 53, 56, 70,
                                                    77, 84, 86, 88, 98, 102, 112, 126, 140, 154, 168, 182, 224},

with maxc Bc = 42, 186, 710, 1778 and |Zk | = 4, 10, 17, 30 respectively.

7.2     The case k = 2
Theorem 7.6. For every d ≥ 0, g2 (d) = 0 or sign g2 (d) = (−1)⌊d/2⌋ . Moreover g2 (d) 6= 0 for all
even d ≥ 42 and all odd d ≥ 19, and the zero set is

                                                     Z2 = {2, 8, 10, 26}.

    Nonvanishing. The two classes c = 0 (n even) and c = 1 (n odd) are one-sign classes (Lemma

B1 = 9 + 2(2 · 2 · 1 + 1) = 19. By Theorem 7.4, g2 (d) 6= 0 for even d ≥ 42 and odd d ≥ 19.

7.3     The value at (k + 2)2 − 3 for even k
Theorem 7.7. Let k ≥ 8 be even and dk = (k + 2)2 − 3.
                       P
     1. gk (dk ) = 2 + s∈Ξk (−1)t (A1 + A2 ) with t = (s2 − 1)/k, and every s ∈ Ξk is odd.
     2. If 8 ∤ k, every t is even and gk (dk ) ≥ 2.
     3. If k = 2pa with p an odd prime (k ≥ 10), then Ξk = ∅ and gk (dk ) = 2.
     4. If k = 2a with a ≥ 3, then Ξk = {k/2−1} and gk (dk ) = 2−A1 −A2 < 0. Values: g8 (97) = −3,
        g16 (321) = −8, g32 (1153) = −31, g64 (4353) = −236, g128 (16897) = −5174.

   Proof. (1) Lemma 6.1 with (−1)k + 1 = 2; for even k, m1 ≡ m2 ≡ t (mod 2), so the bracket is
(−1)t (A1 + A2 ). A root s of s2 ≡ 1 (mod k) with k even is odd.

    (2) s odd gives 8 | s2 − 1 = tk, and v2 (k) ≤ 2 forces v2 (t) ≥ 1. All pair terms are ≥ 0.

    (3) Modulo pa the roots are ±1 and modulo 2 the root is 1, so s2 ≡ 1 (mod 2pa ) implies s ≡ ±1
        (mod 2pa ): only s = 1, k − 1, which are excluded from Ξk .



                                                              26
 (4) The solutions of x2 ≡ 1 (mod 2a ), a ≥ 3, are ±1 and 2a−1 ± 1. So Ξk = {s} with s =
     k/2 − 1 (> 1), and t = (s−1)(s+1)k      = (k/2−2)(k/2)
                                                      k      = k4 − 1, which is odd since k/4 is even.
     Hence gk (dk ) = 2 − (A1 + A2 ). Here n2 = k − s = k2 + 1 and m2 = 4 + 2s − t = 3k           4 + 3,
     which is odd (as 3k  4 is even).  For k  ≥   16 and o ∈ {1, 3, 5}, the pair λ  = {o}, µ = {m 2 − o}
     is admissible in (4.2) for An2 (m2 ): o ≤ n2 ; m2 − o is even; m2 − o ≤ 4 + 2 ≤ k + 2 = 2n2 ;
                                                                                   3k

     and m2 − o > k2 + 1 = n2 iff o < k4 + 2, true for o ≤ 5 when k ≥ 16. These are three different
     pairs, so A2 ≥ 3, A1 ≥ 0 and gk (dk ) ≤ −1. For k = 8 the terms of g8 (97) are (1, 12, +1),
     (3, 11, −2), (5, 9, −3), (7, 6, +1), (9, 2, 0), with sum −3. The values for k = 16, . . . , 128 are
     exact evaluations (Appendix A.1). ■

   Scope. Lemma 7.1 shows that for 4 | k there are classes c in which two roots n, n′ have n2 6≡ n′2
(mod 2k); in such a class the terms of gk (d) have different signs and Theorem 7.4 does not apply.
For k = 4, 8, 12 these classes are: c ≡ 0 (k = 4), c ≡ 1 (k = 8), c ≡ 0, 4 (k = 12) (computed by
enumeration of n modulo 2k). Nothing is claimed for such classes.


8     Parity dominance for odd k ≤ 33
Throughout this section k is odd, 3 ≤ k ≤ 33, and d is allowed for k (Definition
                                                                         P       3.3). The notation
an (m), An (x), Ed , Od is that of Section 4: an (m) = |ϕn (m)|, An (x) = m an (m)xm is the product
(4.1), and by (4.4)
                                                     X                    X
                (−1)d gk (d) = Ed − Od ,      Ed =       an (mn ), Od =      an (mn ).
                                                   n even                 n odd


8.1    Statement
For k in the table of Section 8.7, Rk < 1 and D0 (k) are the numbers computed there (Table 8.2).
Put ηt = 10−6 .
   Theorem 8.1 (parity dominance). Let k ∈ {3, 5, . . . , 33}.

    1. For every allowed d ≥ D0 (k): Od > 0 and Ed ≤ (Rk + ηt ) Od . Consequently (−1)d gk (d) ≤
       −(1 − Rk − ηt )Od < 0; in particular gk (d) 6= 0 and sign gk (d) = (−1)d+1 .
    2. Zk is a finite set, max Zk < D0 (k), and Zk is the explicit set of Appendix B.1 (cardinalities
       and maxima in Table 8.5).
    3. OccFiltk (∞) = Sk : the residue classes mod k that occur for all large d are exactly the squares
       mod k.

    Part 3 follows from Part 2 and Proposition 3.4. Part 2 follows from Part 1 and the finite
certificate of Section 8.7. Part 1 is proved in Sections 8.2 to 8.5: it combines (a) a local limit
estimate for a family of integer-valued random variables attached to An (Sections 8.2, 8.3), (b) a
comparison of each even term with the two odd terms n ± k (Section 8.4) and (c) an upper bound
for the even terms far from the saddle, and a lower bound for one odd term (Section 8.5).
    Computer-assisted steps: the finite computations of Algorithms C (cells), R (block bound), T
(tail) and the modular certificate. Each is specified below and reproduced by the code listed in
Appendix A.3; they use interval (ball) arithmetic with the Arb library through python-flint [21], at
128-bit working precision, except for the modular certificate, which uses exact integer arithmetic
modulo two primes.

    Algorithm C (cells). Fix n0 = 1467. For i = 19, . . . , 331 let ui be the double-precision
number nearest i/100. The 312 cells are Ui = [ui , ui+1 ], i = 19, . . . , 330; they are contiguous and
cover [u19 , u331 ]. For each cell: compute the nine enclosures by Algorithm E (Qe = 600); for each
τ0 ∈ {0.05i : i = 2, . . . , 24} test the hypotheses of Proposition 8.11 and compute δ(τ0 ); keep the
admissible τ0 with least δ. All operations are in ball arithmetic at 128 bits; δ is rounded up to
double precision. All later uses of the cells take u-intervals whose endpoints are double-precision
numbers inside [u19 , u331 ].
    Theorem 8.12 (result of Algorithm C). All 312 cells admit an admissible τ0 . The following
hold: max δ = 0.08727 (on [3.30, 3.31]), min δ = 0.02847; τ0 ∈ [0.10, 0.55]; max D1 = 0.06539,
max D2 = 2.21 · 10−3 , max D3 = 1.99 · 10−2 ; max c = 1.1870. Consequently the conclusion
of Proposition 8.11 holds with n0 = 1467 on every cell. The cell-wise values are stored in

                                                     32
data/pd/cells56_sharp.json (Appendix A.3), and Table 8.4 of Section 8.7 lists the first and
last cells.
    The proof is the computation. (The code is code/pd/clt56.py, make_cells56.py; a full
regeneration produced byte-identical data, see Appendix A.3.)

     Algorithm R. Input k, UA , UB (Table 8.3), H = 0.0025, the cells of Theorem 8.12. Let sb be
the upper end of the ball 1/N1 , γ the upper end of (1−ksb )−3 , nbox = round((UB −UA )/H), and for
i < nbox the box Ui = [UA +iH, UA +(i+1)H] (the last one ends at UB ). For each box compute δU ,
Sb , Sε from the cells (an exact comparison of double-precision endpoints; the program asserts that
the cells meeting the range are contiguous and cover it), enclose Λε , ∆µε , ∆σε by interval arithmetic
in u ∈ Ui , s ∈ [0, sb ] (the 2k terms t ∈ T± are summed with interval arithmetic in the variables
(u, s)), check βε > 0, and compute RUi (rounded up). Return Rk = maxi RUi (rounded up).
     Proposition 8.14 (block bound). For each odd k ∈ [3, 33] Algorithm R succeeds (all βε >
0, all required cells exist) and returns the value Rk of Table 8.2; in all cases Rk < 1 − 10−6 .
Consequently, for d allowed and every even n ≥ N1 admissible for d with un ∈ [UA , UB ]: en ≤
Rk · 21 (on−k + on+k ), and

gives s ≤ sb ). Every odd N occurs

8.6   Proof of Theorem 8.1
Part 1. Let k √  be odd, 3 ≤ k ≤ 33, d ≥ D0 (k) allowed. The even n with n2 ≤ d, k | d − n2 are
split by α = n/ d. The terms with α ∈ [α1 , α2 ] satisfy n ≥ N1 and un ∈ [UA , UB ] (Lemma 8.18),
so by Proposition 8.14 their sum is ≤ Rk Od . The remaining terms are bounded by Proposition
8.19 by ηt Od ; the term n = 0 vanishes for d > 0. Therefore Ed ≤ (Rk + ηt )Od . By Lemma 8.17,
Od ≥ o∗ > 0. With (4.4), (−1)d gk (d) = Ed − Od ≤ −(1 − Rk − ηt )Od < 0 (Table 8.2: Rk < 0.906).
    Part 2 (finite range). Section 8.7 describes the modular certificate for 0 ≤ d ≤ 6 · 107 and the
exact evaluation of the candidates, giving Table 8.5 and Appendix B.1: the set of allowed d ≤ 6·107
with gk (d) = 0. Since 6 · 107 > D0 (k) for each k, Part 1 and the finite computation together give
Zk in full; and max Zk < D0 (k) (Table 8.5).
    Part 3. By Proposition 3.4.

    Proof. An (x)(1 + (−x)n ) = An−1 (x) by (4.1); compare coeﬀicients of xm . The identity for G is

8.8      Computer-assisted steps and what each relies on
The following steps are carried out by programs; everything else in Section 8 is proved by hand
above.

     1. Lattice-sum enclosures (Algorithm E) for the 312 cells and for the window points: Arb
        ball arithmetic, 128 bits, through python-flint [21].
     2. Cells (Algorithm C, Theorem 8.12): the same arithmetic. Output: δ for 312 cells.


                                                                 39
    3. Block bound (Algorithm R, Proposition 8.14), for 16 values of k and about (UB −UA )/0.0025
       boxes each: interval arithmetic over Arb balls.
    4. Tail bound (Algorithm T, Propositions 8.19, 8.20): Arb balls, with the dilogarithm of Arb.
    5. Modular certificate (Algorithm M) and the exact evaluation of Ck (Theorem 8.22): integer
       arithmetic only (a C program with 64-bit residues; exact Python integers).

    The only places where floating-point numbers enter as inputs are: the double-precision endpoints
of cells and boxes (each is an exactly representable binary number, converted without rounding to
a ball), the choice of τ0 , uc , UA , . . . , A0 (any value is valid; correctness never depends on how they
were chosen), and output roundings, which are all directed (upward for upper bounds, downward
for lower bounds). The programs were re-run from their inputs for this paper: every output file
(window parameters, cells, constants, results) is identical to the stored one (Appendix A.3). This
checks reproducibility, not independence of the method.

8.9    Remarks
An independent derivation of the block step (separate code, a different decomposition of the circle
into five regimes, cells of width 0.005 on [0.40, 2.90], a fourth-derivative remainder) reports, for
k = 27, 29, 31, 33, block ratios 0.79055, 0.80677, 0.82294, 0.83906, below the values Rk of Table 8.2.
These numbers play no role in the proofs above and were not recomputed for this paper.
    What is proved and what is not. Theorem 8.1 is proved for the sixteen values k = 3, . . . , 33
through the finite data above. It is a statement about each fixed k; no bound uniform in k is
claimed, and no extension to k ≥ 35 or to even k is asserted.


Part II
The Bridge Series C
9     Introduction to Part II
9.1    Setting and question
The series studied here arise as the “Bridge Series C” of the author’s S2 -11DM2 ET-X programme.
The question posed there was whether C satisfies a residual law: a modular part plus a controlled
remainder, or an exact weight- 12 slash relation. This part is self-contained. All objects are defined
from scratch in §10, and nothing in it depends on, or is claimed to bear on, other parts of that
programme.

9.2    Summary
In one sentence: C and Cthird share the                orth                                  orth is
                                        √ remainder Ps ; up to theta and mock pieces, Ps
one ray class partial theta series of Q( 35); its regulator-type singularity rules out every scalar
modular or mock residual law (Baker); and the obstruction is resolved not by a scalar law but by
a vector-valued weight-1 quantum modular structure, coming from a Siegel theta lift to weight-0
Maass forms, whose cocycle is computed in closed form. The precise statements are collected in
§11.




                                                    40
9.3    What is not claimed
No claim is made about the twin prime conjecture or the Riemann hypothesis, and nothing here
concerns any termination claim of the wider programme. We do not claim that C, Cthird or
Psorth is modular, mock modular or quantum modular as a scalar q-series. The quantum-modular
statements concern the fundamental-domain cone-sum vector of §15. The K3 statements of §26
concern a shared representation, not a shared modular form.

9.4    Organisation
Section 10 fixes conventions and notation, and §11 states the main theorems. Sections 12–18
prove them: the ray class decomposition, the radial asymptotics and the Baker obstruction, the
profile law and the theta lift, the Lewis–Zagier period formula, the Eisenstein/cusp split, the
Herglotz closed form, and the L(0) data for the cusp rows. Sections 19–21 treat the lift family for
general s (tensor structure, dimensions, the cusp content of uL ). Sections 22–25 treat Hs , the cone,
Cthird and the dependence on s. Section 26 gives the K3 realisation, §27 the uniqueness of the
vector completion, and §28 the obstructions for coeﬀicient-emitting maps. Sections 29–32 collect
consequences, numerical checks, dependencies and open problems. Appendix C lists the scripts
that carry out the exact and numerical verifications.


10 Conventions and notation
10.1    Conventions on status
Every result carries one of two labels.
 • Category A: proved in this paper; or exact, meaning a finite verification in rational, integer,
   modular or cyclotomic arithmetic over a stated finite set (including exact ranks, and ranks
   modulo a prime ` together with a stated argument that transfers them to characteristic 0); or
   proved conditional on a named published theorem, stated each time. Where a written proof
   is long and only its structure is reproduced here, it is marked “Proof (outline)”.

 • Category B: statements whose only support is floating-point or multiprecision numerics (in-
   cluding singular-value ranks, FFT character counts and fits), PSLQ recognition, identification
   by citation only, interpretation, analogy, patterns observed on finitely many cases, and con-
   jectures.
Numerical checks reported inside a Category A status box are supporting evidence for a statement
that is proved or exact. The scripts behind every exact and numerical claim are listed in Ap-
pendix C. Section 31 lists the cited results on which proofs depend. Computer-assisted proofs. A
finite verification carried out in rigorous ball (interval) arithmetic (Arb), in which every quantity
entering the final inequality is a proven enclosure of the exact value, counts as Category A and is la-
belled “Category A (computer-assisted)”. Naming. Results are numbered by section, continuously
with the rest of the paper.

10.4    Notation for the lift family
For an integer s ≥5 let As = ( 2s ss ), Qs (n, k) = n2 +snk + 2s k(k +1), a = A−1    s

 • ns := s(s − 2) (the radicand: 35, 48, 99 at s = 7, 8, 11); Ds is the discriminant of the order of
                                          √
   the form (140, 48, 396); εs = (s − 1) + ns .

 • Ns is the number of labels of the lift family; Ns = 4ns for odd s (so N7 = 140).

   of the constant-term vector, dEis := dim VEis , dcusp := dim V − dEis ; V ⊥ is the complement of
   V in WP .

 • N denotes the negation map uα,β 7→ u−α,−β on labels (not Nto be confused with Ns ); Zp is the
   local reflection x 7→ −x on a p-primary factor, and Z(m) = p|m Zp .

 • Wp± are the even and odd parts of a local Weil representation; A3 , B1 are the 3- and 1-
   dimensional summands of the 2-adic factor ρ2 (Theorem 19.3); X ± = W5± ⊗ W7± at s = 7.

 • Hs always denotes the cone series of §10 with the fixed walls c1 = (−1, 1), c2 = (−4, 1) (for
   every s). The axes-cone series Hsaxes of §23 is a separate object. We write g := gcd(24, s − 8)
   and ∆ := (s − 2)(7s − 32).

Theorem 11.4 (Gauss-mean pattern and zero set). For gcd(h,      √ m) = 1: G(h/m) = 0 if 2 | m
or 5 | m; |G(h/m)| = 1/m if gcd(m, 70) = 1; |G(h/m)| = 7/m if 7 | m and gcd(m, 10) = 1.

105. The limit follows from leading-order Euler–Maclaurin (Proposition 13.1); it is checked numerically
down to t = 6.25 · 10−4 .

Theorem 11.5 (Baker obstruction: scalar no-go). (i) (Additive.) There is no identity S =
    F + E with S ∈ {C, Cthird }, F in the Q-span M of §13 (holomorphic modular forms with
    algebraic coeﬀicients, signature-(1, 1) Zwegers holomorphic parts with rational characteristics,
    unary/partial/false theta series), and E(e−t ) = o(1/t).

 (ii) (Multiplicative.) For holomorphic modular forms g 6≡ 0 and F of any weights, levels and
      multipliers, with complex coeﬀicients, and any q r factor: Cthird · g 6= F and C · g 6= F .

(iii) (Scalar W7 .) For no k and no constant λ is C(τ ) − λ(7τ )−k C(−1/(7τ )) radially bounded at
      all rationals.

Status (Category A). (i) is proved conditional on Baker’s theorem [1] and on the q-expansion principle
(algebraicity of expansions
                    √       at all cusps),
                                   √       both
                                             √ cited. (ii) is proved; for C the nonvanishing input is the
closed form aC = π(833 + 204 14 − 14 1190)/2380 6= 0 (Theorem 11.3). (iii) is an exact finite
computation: 178 of 270 test rationals (denominator < 30) have exactly one of G(x), G(−1/(7x)) zero,
including x = 5/7; the zero pattern is that of Theorem 11.4.

Theorem 11.8 (Eisenstein/cusp split). uL = ua,0 and uR are pure Eisenstein (combinations of
the 8 genus series attached to ψ ◦ N, ψ mod 20). The 42-dimensional function span V splits
SL2 (Z)-equivariantly as VEis ⊕ Vcusp with dim VEis = 36 (from the 116 Eisenstein components) and
dim Vcusp = 6 (from the 24 cusp components, §16). The cusp components are exact √     combinations
of 12 dihedral Maass forms (λ = 41 ) from the ray class characters of conductor (2 35) of pure
infinity type; these 12 forms occur only in 6 fixed linear combinations. Consequently ψcusp ≡ 0 for
the scalar cocycle.
Status (Category A). uL is Eisenstein by a hand proof independent of PARI (§16.1), with an exact
coeﬀicient match for all n ≤ 60 000. The dimensions 36 and 6 and the S, T -invariance of the split are
exact: VEis = A3 ⊗ X + and Vcusp = B1 ⊗ X − (Proposition 19.4). ψcusp ≡ 0 follows because ψ involves
only uL and uR .

Category B. The expression of the cusp components through the 12 named dihedral forms rests on
floating-point fits (§16.2); the rank 6 of the 12 forms inside the cusp components is floating, and is
explained exactly by dim(B1 ⊗ X − ) = 6. Cuspidality ⇔ χ 6= χ ◦ σ, the level 19600, the nebentypus and
newform status are standard theory [11], cited.

12 The real-quadratic decomposition and the ray class
12.1    Elementary identities (Theorem 11.1)
Proof of Theorem 11.1. (i). In q 8 f2,7,7 (−q 10 , −q 14 , q) put n = r + 1, k = s + 1. The signs (−1)r+s
from x = −q 10 , y = −q 14 cancel those in f , and on r, s ≥ 0 the exponent is 8 + 10r + 14s + r(r −
1) + 7rs + 72 s(s − 1) = Q− (n, k); this orthant gives Cthird . On r, s < 0, after (r, s) = (−1 − n, −1 − k)
the exponent is Q7 (n, k) with sign −1; this gives −Psorth .
    (ii) is (i) combined with C = Psorth − Hs . For (iii): in the second sum defining Θhol substitute
(n, k) = P (−n′ , −k ′ ) with n′ ≥ 0, k ′ ≥ 1; since Q− (−v) = Q7 (v)    Pit equals    R. The first sum is
                    2                                                             2
Cthird + n≥1 q n (split k ≥ 1 and k = 0). Hence Θhol = Cthird + n≥1 q n − R. The k = 0 row of
                             P       2
Psorth gives Psorth = R + n≥0 q n = R + 12 (1 + θ3 ), and then (i) gives Θhol = q 8 f + θ3 . (iv) is the
definition combined with (ii).

Status (Category A). All identities in Theorem 11.1 were in addition checked with exact rational
coeﬀicients through q 3000 ; the Hs summation box was enlarged by 30 with identical output.


                                                          46
Remark 12.1 (Origin of Cmixed ). At q 294 the point (21, −1) lies on the c2 -wall n + 21k = 0, has
w = 12 and lies outside the orthant, contributing − 21 . Points such as (22, −1) (q 330 ) and (−22, 1)
(q 337 ) lie in the Hs cones but outside the orthant. Thus “C = Caxis + Cthird ” holds only through
q 293 . The coeﬀicient set {0, 1, 32 } holds through q 101 only (first coeﬀicient 2 at q 102 , first 3 at q 144 ,
first 21 at q 294 ); through q 3000 the set is {0, 21 , 1, 23 , 2, 3}. The support {0, 1, 2, 4} mod 7 holds for all
terms since Q7 ≡ n2 (mod 7). (Category A, exact.)

    Consequently, every part of C and of Cthird other than Psorth is a signature-(1, 1) mock/indefinite
theta object or a unary theta series, and the residual-law question is a question about Psorth alone.
Note also that R is a cone sum of the indefinite form Q7 (positive only   P on the closed orthant), so
it cannot be split by inclusion–exclusion into full lattice theta series: Z2 q Q7 diverges.

13 Asymptotics and the Baker obstruction
13.1    Two-dimensional Euler–Maclaurin
All radial expansions in this paper rest on the following expansion for cone sums.
Proposition 13.1 (Cone Euler–Maclaurin). For every rational x, the shifted-lattice cone sums of
e−tQ that make up Hs (e(x)e−t ) and EL (x + h) have a full asymptotic expansion in powers of t1/2 .
The coeﬀicients are area terms, edge Bernoulli sums with the half-weight rule B̃r at the edges, and
corner terms. The remainders are explicit integrals of periodic Bernoulli functions and are bounded
uniformly in every sector | arg t| ≤ π/2 − δ.

Status (Category A). Proved, by a one-dimensional lemma (Zagier’s asymptotic lemma with the
                                 p+1 R ∞
Euler–Maclaurin remainder (−1)  p!    0
                                         B̃p (y − u)f (p) (y) dy), a quadrant lemma, and a decomposition
of the closed cone into translated quadrants. The sector-uniform bound is order-3 two-variable Euler–
Maclaurin with every remainder bounded; only |B̃p | enters the bounds. The written proof is in the
companion report (Appendix C); its structure is reproduced here.
   In particular we use the following consequence, which we call R1: for rational x, any cone sum
f of this type and | arg t| ≤ π/2 − δ, |t| ≤ 1,
                                f (e(x)e−t ) = A/t + h t−1/2 + κ + O(|t|1/2 ),
with the coeﬀicients of the real-t expansion. Thus radial expansions hold non-tangentially.

Lemma 13.2 (Zwegers parts carry no 1/t term). The holomorphic part of any signature-(1, 1)
                  c1 ,c2
indefinite theta ϑa,b    with c1 , c2 of negative norm and rational a, b has 1/t coeﬀicient 0 at q = e−t .
This covers Hs , Θhol and q 8 f2,7,7 .

Sketch. The weight is +1 on a cone C and −1 on −C (walls contribute O(t−1/2 )). The mean of
e(B(ν, b)) over the coset is the same on C and −C, and Q is even, so the leading integrals cancel.

Status (Category A). Proved (symmetry argument; null cones excluded). Numerically t Hs → 0 (fit
−0.0002).


13.3      The additive obstruction (Theorem 11.5(i))

used is R1 (§13.1).

Category B. Reading: the remainder of C is “weight-1 sized” at q → 1 with a regulator as coeﬀicient;
weight- 12 false thetas are O(t−1/2 ) and cannot carry it. An Appell–Lerch formula for E or Psorth with
algebraic data would likewise be excluded, but this needs the q → 1 asymptotics of Appell–Lerch sums,
which were not verified; that obstruction is conditional.

Proof. Along q = e−t , F/g = c t−j e−α/t (1 + O(e−β/t )) times a power series in t, so only integer-step
powers of t occur; but Cthird has the nonzero term −aP t−1/2 and C the nonzero term aC t−1/2
(Table 13).

Status (Category A). Proved. For C the nonvanishing of aC rests on its closed form (Theorem 11.3).
This covers every space with k ∈ { 12 , 1, 32 , 2} and N | 140 or 280; no Sturm-bound search is needed.

   For the scalar W7 question: a singularity ∼ G(x)I/(2πε′ ) at x (with τ = x + iε′ ) maps under
W7 to ∼ |7x|2 G(−1/(7x))I/(2πε′ ), so boundedness of C − λ(7τ )−k C(−1/(7τ )) would force G(x) =
λ(7x)−k |7x|2 G(−1/(7x)) for all x. This fails at the 178 zero-mismatch points; on the 60 pairs of
common support the moduli obey an exact weight-1 relation with |λ| = 7−1/2 but the phase varies
(π/2 at 1/7 and 1, −π/6 at 1/3 and 3/7); for W35 , W70 , W140 there are no pairs with both values
nonzero. The map τ 7→ −1/(7τ ) does not normalise Γ0 (70).
Status (Category A). Zero/nonzero pattern and moduli: exact, by Theorem 11.4 (the floating-point
exponential sums, nonzero values ≥ 0.034 and zeros < 10−9 , agree).

Category B. The phase values quoted above are floating-point (with a wide margin) and are not
separately proved.

Category B. The scalar failure is vector mixing: the leading profiles obey a vector-valued law (§14.1),
under which W7 -type maps mix components; this is not a contradiction with modularity of the vector.


14 The theta lift and the vector-valued S/T law
14.1   Leading order: radial profiles
For α, β ∈ Q2 let Gα,β (x) be the mean over ν ∈ α + Z2 of e(xQ(ν) + B(ν, β)); this is the 1/t radial
profile (divided by the cone measure) of any cone sum over that coset and twist (leading-order
Euler–Maclaurin, as in Theorem 11.4 and R1). For rational α, β and rational x the summand is
periodic on α + Z2 , and the mean is the average over one period. Write A∗ = (A11 , A22 ) = (2, 7),
so 21 A−1 A∗ = ( 12 , 0).

15.3   The branch lemma
For z ∈ C \ iR write ς(z) = sgn Re z.

Lemma 15.2 (Branch lemma). Let z ∈ H\iR and let Cz be the image of the vertical ray −1/z+iR≥0
under w′ 7→ −1/w′ , an arc from z to 0. On the ray take the positive root of R−1/z ; on Cz take the
root of Rz continued from the principal root on iR>0 near 0. Then, for w ∈ Cz ,

                                  R−1/z (−1/w)1/2 = ς(z) z Rz (w)1/2 .

Proof. Since −1/w + 1/z = (w − z)/(wz) and Im(−1/w) = Im w/|w|2 , one has R−1/z (−1/w) =
z 2 Rz (w). Both roots are continuous and nonvanishing along Cz , so the two sides agree up to a
sign that is constant on Cz . Compare at w → 0 along Cz , where Cz is tangent to iR. There
Rz (w) = Im w z −2 (1 + o(1)), and the principal root of z −2 is ς(z)/z (the root with positive real
part), so ς(z) z Rz (w)1/2 = (Im w)1/2 (1 + o(1)) > 0. On the other side −1/w → i∞ on the ray,
where R−1/z (−1/w) > 0 and its positive root is taken. Hence the sign is +.

Status (Category A). Elementary. Checked numerically at points of both half-planes (the sign is +1
for Re z > 0 and −1 for Re z < 0).

small arcs around z contribute o(1); the contribution at height Y → ∞ is O(1/Y ); at 0 the arc Cz
is tangent to iR and uR decays exponentially. Hence

The automorphy factor. With the holomorphic factor x in place of |x| the cocycle at x < 0 is
unbounded (it grows like 1/y); the bounded object on the negative side is Ψ− , i.e. the real factor
|x|. Numerically ψ(−x) = ψ(x) to 8 · 10−14 (Category B; this symmetry is not proved here and
is not used). Near 0, ψ(x) ≈ −0.0549i x2 + 0.0206 x3 (from x = 0.01, 0.05); ψ is not analytic
at 0. Since T acts by phases and S, T generate, every hγ is real-analytic off γ −1 ∞. Example:
ψ(1) = 0.006883993760419869190321587 − 0.03825971221697307224258855 i.
Category B. Interpretation: the 72-dimensional fundamental-domain vector is a vector-valued quantum
modular form of weight 1 in Zagier’s sense for the real automorphy factor |cx + d| (“weight ( 21 , 12 )”), i.e.
the f -function of a λ = 14 Maass vector, with exceptional set {0} for S; E itself is then a “renormalised”
weight-1 quantum modular object with the singular parts I · G(x) removed. The Lewis–Zagier period-
function reading [10] and the broader context of [4] are cited for context only.

16.2     The Eisenstein/cusp split (36 + 6) and the dihedral forms
Every one of the 140 components is exactly a ray class theta series modulo m = p32 p5 p7 ∞1 ∞2
(fit over n coprime to 70 up to 54 880; max residual 4.4 · 10−14 over 280 fits). Expanding in
characters: mixed-type content ≤ 10−29 ; 116 components have cusp content ≤ 3 · 10−30 (pure
Eisenstein); 24 components have Eisenstein content ≤ 8 · 10−31 (pure cusp). Function-space di-
mensions. Since distinct frequencies give independent mode functions, dim span{uα,β } is the rank
of the label×frequency coeﬀicient matrix. This rank is 42; the Eisenstein components span 36
and the cusp components 6 dimensions, and both subspaces are S- and T -invariant. These state-
ments are exact (Proposition 19.4); the floating-point rank computations agree (invariance residuals
≤ 8 · 10−16 ; with independent code at |Q| ≤ 12 and |Q| ≤ 25, gap 6.4 versus 6 · 10−15 ). The 24
cusp components are the β = −a labels with 70α1 ∈ {7, 21, 49, 63} and 7 | denominator of α2 ,
excluding the four labels 70α = (49, 56), (63, 42), (7, 28), (21, 14), which give identically zero func-
tions. In all, 11 labels (all with β = −a) give identically vanishing uα,β . This vanishing is exact:
for the first five (α ∈ {( 10  7 4        9 3
                                , 5 ), ( 10           1 2
                                            , 5 ), ( 10           3 1
                                                        , 5 ), ( 10 , 5 ), ( 21 , 0)}) the automorph γ, for the remaining
six (α = ( 12 , 7j ), 1 ≤ j ≤ 6; also j = 0) the automorph −γ, maps α + Z2 onto itself, commutes
with γ 2 , leaves the kernel of the lift unchanged (it depends on ν evenly and γ-covariantly, and the
closed geodesic is γ-stable), and multiplies the phase e(B(ν, β)) by −1 (because, with g = ±γ the
relevant automorph, g −1 β − β = µ ∈ L′ with B(α, µ) ≡ 12 ); so the sum over ν modulo γ 2 equals
its own negative. Checked exactly (rational arithmetic) over all 140 labels: these are exactly 11
labels. (Category A.)

16.3    Function span, S and T spectra
Let V = span{uα,β (w)} ⊂ C140 be the span of the values of the vector u (equivalently the column
span of the coeﬀicient matrix). Since u(−1/w) = Su(w) and u(w + 1) = T u(w), V is S, T -invariant,
and V ⊂ Fix(P ).
Proposition 16.1. (i) dim V = 42 = 36 + 6, V = VEis ⊕ Vcusp , both summands S, T -invariant.
(ii) On Fix(P ) (dim 72), S has eigenvalues ±1 with multiplicities 36/36 (tr S|Fix(P ) = 0); on V
they are 21/21, namely 18/18 on VEis and 3/3 on Vcusp . (iii) T has order 140. On Fix(P ) its
eigenvalues are e(r) with 36 distinct r ∈ 140        1
                                                       Z/Z, of multiplicities 1 (12 values), 2 (18 values) and
                  19 31 59 111 131 139
4 (6 values: r = 140 , 140 , 140 , 140 , 140 , 140 ). On VEis each of the 36 values occurs once; on Vcusp the
eigenvalues are the 6 values of multiplicity 4. Hence Fix(P ) = V ⊕ V ⊥ with a 30-dimensional T -
stable complement V ⊥ (multiplicities 0, 1, 2), which the lift functions do not realise. (iv) The 24 cusp
characters form 12 σ-orbits of size 2 and give 12 distinct dihedral forms, all with nonzero content;
the 24 × 48 content matrix has rank 6, so the 12 forms occur only in 6 fixed linear combinations.

Status (Category A). (i) by Proposition 19.4; (ii) by the exact Gauss-sum traces of the local factors
(Theorem 19.3), which give n± = d/2 on each of the four sectors; (iii) order 140 exact (monomial
T , rational phases), multiplicities from the exact phases and the sector decomposition. Floating-point
computations agree (singular-value gap 17.1 → 1.8 · 10−13 , invariance residuals ≤ 8 · 10−16 ).

Category B. (iv): the rank 6 of the content matrix is floating-point. Its value is explained exactly
by Vcusp = B1 ⊗ X − (dim = 6). The complement V ⊥ = B1 ⊗ X + ⊕ A3 ⊗ X − is killed by the lift
exactly (Proposition 19.4); the positive-frequency partners fα,β span the same V . Weight-1 vectors lie
in Fix(−P ) (Remark 25.3(e)), so they cannot realise V ⊥ either.


Consequence for ψ. Both representations of the scalar ψ involve only uL and uR , which are
Eisenstein; hence ψcusp ≡ 0, ψ = ψEis (Category A given the above). The 24 cusp rows of Φ are
nonzero (e.g. Φ73 (1) = 0.120446470003 + 0.176437422094 i); their identification as Lewis–Zagier
period functions of the cusp vector is Category B.

(for s = 7 these are the 140 functions of Theorem 11.6).

Remark 19.1 (The S versus S −1 label convention). Let Slab be the label matrix of Theorem 11.6,
i.e. u(−1/w) = Slab u(w). For s = 7, 8, 11 one checks exactly: Slab  2 = P , (S T )3 = I on all labels,

Remark 19.2 (Label operators). Label maps (negation, γ, the local reflections Zp ) are realised
as monomial matrices Y with Y [i, j(i)] = e(phi ), the convention of the permutation matrix of
the family. With this convention neg = ρ(−I) = S 2 , and neg, γ, Zp (p | s) and Zq (q | s − 2)
commute exactly with S and T (exact exponent arithmetic modulo 2D2 at s = 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 25).
(Category A.)

Proof outline. Every entry of Slab is one term e(r)/ 35 with r ∈ Q, and T is monomial, so (i) is a
finite identity over Q(ζ140 ), checked exactly. For (ii), (Fp Tp )3 = c Zp exactly in Z[ζp ] with c2 = 125
(p = 5) and c2 = −343 (p = 7); with Gauss’s sign of the quadratic Gauss sum this gives the scalars
γ5 = −1, γ7 = e(3/4), so ρp is exactly the Weil representation of qp ; by the congruence theorem for
Weil representations of finite quadratic modules of odd level [16], ρp factors through SL2 (Z/p). ρ2

                                                                     −1
48 elements. The scalars cancel in the product, which equals (Slab      , T ). (iv) and (v) follow from the
local Gauss sums.

Status (Category A). Exact (rational and cyclotomic arithmetic), conditional on the cited congruence
theorem for the odd local factors [16] (hypotheses: exact match with the Weil formulas, odd level). The
local forms are determined as follows: a 140th-root exponent a decomposes as a = 35x4 + 28x5 + 20x7 ,
i.e. x4 ≡ 3a mod 4, x5 ≡ 2a mod 5, x7 ≡ 6a mod 7, which gives q5 = 2x2 /5 and q7 = x2 /7. (Since 2 is a
non-square mod 5 and 3 is a non-square mod 7, these are not isometric to x2 /5 and 3x2 /7.)

Proposition 19.4 (V is the matched-sector space; local reflections). Let√X ± = W5± ⊗ W7± . At
s = 7: (i) negation acts on u′ as Z5 ⊗ Z7 ⊗ I4 , and the unit γ (ε = 6 + 35) acts as I5 ⊗ Z7 ⊗
diag(1, 1, 1, −1); (ii) V = Fix(N ) ∩ Fix(γ) = A3 ⊗ X + ⊕ B1 ⊗ X − , of dimension 36 + 6 = 42, and
the lift kills V ⊥ = B1 ⊗ X + (12) ⊕ A3 ⊗ X − (18); (iii) VEis = A3 ⊗ X + and the cuspidal part of V is
Vcusp = B1 ⊗ X − . The 24 cusp components of §16 are exactly the nonzero labels with (, δ) = (1, 1),
and the 11 identically vanishing labels are exactly the 6 inconsistent orbits (x5 x7 = 0,  = δ = 1);
(iv) each of the 42 classes is a free hN, γi-orbit (sizes 1, 2, 4: 3, 15, 24 classes), with proportionality
constants ±1 in the u′ basis; (v) the local reflections Z5 , Z7 and Z2 := diag(1, 1, 1, −1) commute
with ρ. Their signs (Z5 , Z7 , Z2 ) are (+, +, +) on VEis , (−, −, −) on Vcusp , (+, +, −) on B1 ⊗ X +
and (−, −, +) on A3 ⊗ X − . Thus Z5 Z7 = +1 on WP (this is P ) and Z7 Z2 = +1 exactly on V (this
is γ). S commutes with all three, and n± = d/2 on every joint eigenspace.
Proof outline. (i) The identities u−α,−β = uα,β and uγα,γβ = uα,β hold as identities of functions,
and their actions on u′ are computed exactly. (ii) Hence V ⊆ Fix(N ) ∩ Fix(γ), which has dimension

                                                    63
42. The exact Fourier matrix (sums of 4900th roots of unity, |Q| ≤ 25) has rank 42 modulo a prime
above ` = 29401 ≡ 1 mod 4900; rank cannot increase under reduction, so dim V ≥ 42. (iii) The
constant-term vector is δx=0 ⊗ (e00 + e01 ), a pure tensor with 2-adic part in A3 ; its cyclic span is
A3 ⊗ X + (the argument is that of Theorem 20.1(iii)). The cuspidal functions of the span are those
orthogonal to VEis (the λ = 41 constant-term criterion); by unitarity this is B1 ⊗ X − .

Status (Category A). Exact: (i), (ii), (iv), (v) and the label statements of (iii). The mechanism that
kills V ⊥ is the γ phase flip: the unit acts by −1 at 7 and by −1 on the odd characteristic, the analogue
of the exact vanishing of the 11 labels (§16.2). Part (iii) uses the cited congruence theorem and the
standard λ = 14 constant-term criterion.

Category B. Calling Z5 , Z7 , Z2 the Atkin–Lehner operators W5 , W7 , W4 on the lifted forms (not checked
on the forms). The Hecke-character reading: a character contributes only if ψ(ε) = ψ(−1) = 1; genus
characters are even at the ramified primes and land in A3 ⊗ X + ; characters odd at 5 and 7 are not genus
characters, hence not σ-invariant, and give Maass cusp forms in B1 ⊗ X − . The identification of Vcusp
with specific dihedral Hecke eigenforms rests on the floating fits of §16.

19.3    Sectors: parity and the orbit lemma
Proposition 19.7 (Odd-parity sectors are cusp). If ρ(T ) has no eigenvalue 1 on a ρ-invariant
subspace σ, the projection of the lift onto σ isQcuspidal. Consequently dcusp ≥ (s − 1)(s − 3)/4
wherever the B1 sector lies in V , and dEis ≤ 3 (pe + 1)/2 whenever no odd parity is carried only
by primes with e ≥ 2 (every tested odd s ≤ 99; the first undecided cases are s = 225, 1225).
                                                                                                Q
Status (Category A). Proved: no constant term at any cusp. Category B: equality dEis = 3            (pe +
1)/2, i.e. that the all-even sector carries no cusp content.

Proposition 19.8 (Parity fact). For every odd s, every ∆ label has even second coordinate x2 .
Hence Zall acts as negation on ∆, fixes every hγ, −1i-orbit, and every sector with εall = −1 vanishes.

Status (Category A). Symbolic: D·A−1 = −2 adjA gives even second coordinates on L′ ; a = (s2 , −2s);
D is even.

Proposition 19.9 (Orbit lemma). For every odd s, cε (O0 , qmin ) = (1 + εall )NO0 (qmin ), where
O0 = {±ν0 , ±γν0 } and ν0 = (s, −2). This is nonzero on every sector with εall = +1. Extra reduced
classes do occur, at s = 27, 245, 363, 847, 2025, . . . (16 cases with s ≤ 30001), but they never land
in ZS O0 .

Status (Category A). Symbolic (Nagell bound, γ 2 ν0 ≡ ν0 , stabiliser {1, Zall }, exclusion of extra
classes). Also checked exactly for odd s ≤ 401 and at all 16 exceptions. At s = 27 the sector run
certifies dim V = 702 (exact), in agreement with Theorem 20.1. Combined with Proposition 19.8, the
matched sectors with εall = +1 have a nonzero lift. Injectivity therefore follows wherever the sector is
irreducible (squarefree s(s − 2)). Open: dim V = s(s − 1) when e ≥ 2.

s = 5, . . . , 51 and for s = 72, the exact rational rank of the integer orbit-count matrix equals the upper
bound (for s = 8, 18, 32, 50, 72 the half-cone bound of (iv)). Hence dim V is known   Q exactly there; for
odd s ≤ 51, dim V = s(s − 1). (iii) Eisenstein dimension. For odd s, dEis = 3 pe ∥ns (pe + 1)/2, at

(iii), odd s: by Theorem 19.5 the constant-term vector (labels with α ∈ Z2 , i.e. x = 0,  = 0) is the
pure tensor δ0 ⊗ · · · ⊗ δ0 ⊗ (e00 + e01 ), with 2-adic part in A3 . Since ρ(SL2 (Z)) is the full product
of the local images, the cyclic span of a pure tensor is the tensor product of the local cyclic spans.
These are A3 (irreducible, dimension 3) and the cyclic span of δ0 in ρqp , of dimension (pe + 1)/2:
by hand for e = 1, and by exact certification (Krylov rank mod ` equal to the O(∆)-orbit upper
bound) for the listed pe . Even s: the family is literally the Weil representation of L′ /Z2 (an exact
identity of the S, T matrices), with local factors certified as stated. (iv): the square root and the
half-cone bound are exact; the s = 8 relations NO12 = NO7 + NO9 and NO2 = NO15 + NO19 are
instances.

2) + 3 E(s)E(s − 2) − (pe + 1)/2 ≥ (s − 1)(s − 3)/4 > 0, with equality iff s and s − 2 are both
prime powers. This reproduces 2, 6, 12, 20, 30, 84, 110, 72, 180, 218, 132 at s = 5, . . . , 25 and 758 at
s = 41. Category A at the certified s; for general odd s it is Category B, through the open lower
bound. The family cusp part is therefore a local (Weil-parity) phenomenon, not a class-group one.

20.2    The case s = 2m2
Let s = 2m2 with m odd and g = (γ + I)/(2m), as in Theorem 20.1(iv).

Proposition 20.3 (Complement trace). For every odd m: #Fix(g) = 2(m − 1) and #Fix(−g) =
2(m + 1) on the complement, tr(g | Fix(N, γ)) = 2m, and the g = −1 eigenspace X of Fix(N ) has
dimension m(m − 1).

Status (Category A). A(g ∓ 1)−1 is integral, and γ = 1 on the complement C. Exact at m = 3, 5, 7
as well.

Proposition 20.4 (Half-cone relations). If g acts on M7 -classes (more generally on the classes
of Z2 + Zt) and m is prime, the span of the half-cone g-relations ec − egc is exactly δiso ⊗ X, of
dimension m(m − 1).

Status (Category A). Proved for prime m, given that g is consistent on the classes. That consistency
is checked exactly only at m = 3, 5, 7. At m = 3, 5 the exact half-cone kernel equals δiso ⊗ X vector by
vector. Open: g-consistency for all odd m, and composite m (the m-part is not cyclic). In general the
kernel can be larger than the g-relations; see Theorem 20.5.

Theorem 20.5 (dim V (98) = 2406). At s = 98 (m = 7), dim V = 2406. The kernel of the lift on
the 98-dimensional 7-adic old space has dimension 44. It is spanned by the 42 = m(m−1) g-relations
and two h-relations, R1 (at q = 11/12) and R2 (at q = 1/4). Here h = (g + I)/4 ∈ SO(A)(Q), with
h2 = g and hT Ah = A. The half-cone upper bound 2408 is not sharp.

Status (Category A). Lower bound: the exact rank is 2406 (flint, Qmax = 40; old-piece ranks are stable
to Qmax = 130). The new pieces are injective, and all 44 missing dimensions lie in the old space. Upper

Corollary 20.7. On V0 , τ has eigenvalues +1, −1, 0 with multiplicities k(k + 1), k(k − 1),
m(m + 1) − 2k 2 ; so tr τ = 2k and rank τ = 2k 2 . The extra relations are explicit: ↑ of the
h̄-odd hḡ, −1i-invariant functions on C1 = N1♯ /N1 . In particular the number of closed orbits is
m(m + 1) = rankg .

Status (Category A). Consequence of Proposition K1.

Proposition 20.8 (Sharpness of Proposition K1 on the old space). For k = 2, 3, 4, 5 (m =
7, 17, 31, 49), the theta lift on F = Fun(Mt♯ /Mt )⟨γ,−1⟩ has rank exactly m(m + 1) − k(k − 1) =
54, 300, 980, 2430. So at these m the old space has no relations beyond the g- and h-relations.

Status (Category A). The upper bound is Proposition K1. The lower bound is the rank over F231 −1 of
the exact (orbit × norm) count matrix of the x ∈ Mt♯ with 0 < |Q(x)| ≤ Qmax , in an exact fundamental
domain for hγi; a rank modulo a prime is a lower bound for the rank over Q. At k = 2 this reproduces
the old-piece ranks 42 + 12 of Theorem 20.5. Not claimed: dim V (578) = 83532, which would also need
injectivity and independence of the new pieces at s = 578.

Status (Category A). Proposition K1: proved for all k ≥ 2. The symbolic-in-k ingredients and the
Burnside counts (brute-forced for n = 2, . . . , 40) are checked by computer. Consistency checks of the
reformulation: exact over Q for k = 2, 3, 4, rank(I + τ ) = 54, 300, 980 with minimal polynomial x3 − x;
modulo two primes at k = 5, 6 (m = 49, 71), extra = 20, 30 and rank τ = 50, 72.



                                                      68
Dimension consequences (Category A as upper bounds; sharpness open). Proposition K1
is a statement about the lattice-level rank rankh , which is a proved upper bound for the old lift
rank. Every h-relation is a proved theta relation (the argument of Theorem 20.5), so for s = 2m2 ,
m = 2k 2 − 1, subtracting the k(k − 1) extra relations gives proved upper bounds: dim V (98) ≤ 2406
(equality, Theorem 20.5) and dim V (578) ≤ 83532, and likewise at every m = 2k 2 − 1. Whether
these bounds are sharp at m = 17, 31, . . . is not proved.

20.3   The case s = 64
Proposition 20.9 (s = 64). dim WP (64) = 1986. The 2-adic cyclic span d2 at k = 6 equals 36, so
the 2-adic factor of the function span is 36 · 16 = 576 at s = 64.

Status (Category A). WP is an exact count. For the upper bound, d2 ≤ #O(∆2 )-orbits = 42, minus
a 6-dimensional S, T -invariant subspace Y ⊥ δ0 that is verified exactly in Z[ζ64 ], leaving 36. The lower
bound 36 is exact. The value 2k−1 + 4 for general k remains Category B.


20.4   Prime-square sectors
For s = p2 the isotropic projector Π splits each sector into an old piece, the p-local invariants, and
a new piece, their complement; both are subrepresentations.
Proposition 20.10 (Prime-square sectors: ranks). Exactly:

                              s   (+, +) old   (+, +) new    (−, −) new    total
                             9           12            48             12     72
                            11           18            72             20    110
                            25           36           432            132    600

Every piece is injective. The ν0 coeﬀicient is nonzero on every new piece and zero on every old
piece.

Status (Category A). Exact integer ranks. The irreducible decompositions are Proposition 20.12; a
uniform proof is open.
    Let s = p2 with p an odd prime. Write Wp± for the even and odd parts of the Weil representation
of Z/p2 (or of Z/q), and A3 for the irreducible 3-dimensional 2-adic factor.

Given the cited factorisation of a Weil representation through Mp2 (Z/nT ), these upper bounds are

21 Cusp content of the vector uL
The family statements of §20 concern the whole span V . Whether the single vector uL = ua,0 has
cusp content is a different question; the two must be kept apart.

Proposition 21.1 (Cusp criterion for uL ). Let G = Cl+           M ∞1 ∞2 with M = 2dc, d = 2(s − 2),
ns = c2 D0 ; let T ⊂ G be the class set of the coprime part of uL , S = Stab(T ), j = [−1],
Ḡ = G/(S + hji), H = (1 − σ)G and H̄ its image in Ḡ. (i) The coprime part of uL is Eisenstein
(genus-pure) iff H̄ = 1, i.e. iff σ acts trivially on Ḡ; the number of non-genus pure characters is then
|Ḡ|−|Ḡσ |. (ii) For odd s with c = 1 (ns squarefree), only the coprime part occurs and Theorem 21.3
                                       √
decides: uL is Eisenstein iff h(Q( ns )) = 2ω(s) . (iii) The verdicts for 5 ≤ s ≤ 44 are those of
Table 17. (iv) The rule “uL has cusp content iff h+ 6= #genera” is false: s = 14, 17, 18, 22, 23 have
h+ = #genera and cusp content (all with H̄ of order 2). (v) The two notions differ: dcusp > 0 at
s = 5, 7, 13, 15, where uL is Eisenstein. The uL verdict is class-group data (H̄) on top of the local
structure.
                                                        1−σ
Status (Category A). (i): reduction (Tate’s ker N = CK      for cyclic K/Q, plus the dictionary “Maass
content of uL = pure-type character content of its class sets”). (iv): exact finite group computations
per s on complete coprime parts (the s = 18 coprime part is complete at B = 150 000).

Category B. Suﬀiciency of the modulus M = 2dc for all norms (stable under M → 2M wherever
tested).
   Here W ⊂ (O/M )× is the unit stabiliser of β0 + L (unrelated to WP ), S0 is the coprime-part
stabiliser and ψ(λ) = [(λ)].

Theorem 21.2 (Coset property). For every s and every c-part c, Tc +ψ(W) = Tc , and S0 = ψ(W).
Hence every Tc is a union of S0 -cosets.

Status (Category A). The lemma Tc + ψ(W) = Tc is elementary. S0 = ψ(W) follows from the local
condition Rp = Wp , proved for all s and p: it reduces to the fact that ratios of exact-valuation elements
of β0 + L lie in the multiplier order of L. The cases are p ∤ f ; odd p | c with p | s or p | s − 2; and p = 2
in each 2-adic case. Also checked exactly: global S0 = ψ(W) at s = 5, . . . , 44, and Rp = Wp at s ≤ 150.

Theorem
    p     21.3 (Squarefree criterion). Let s be odd with s(s − 2) squarefree, and h =
h(Q( s(s − 2))). Then only the coprime c-part occurs, |Ḡσ | = 2ω(s) ϕ(s − 2), and uL is Eisenstein
⇐⇒ h = 2ω(s) . The number of non-genus characters is ϕ(s − 2)(h − 2ω(s) ).

Status (Category A). The proof uses Chevalley’s ambiguous class number formula (ray form, unit-
norm index 1) and, independently, Gauss genus theory; these are classical theorems applied to exact
data. The exact cross-check covers 47 values s ≤ 151: Eisenstein only at s = 5, 7, 13, 15.

Status (Category A). Verdicts are per s over all (infinitely many) c-parts, using Theorem 21.2 and
